# MATRYOSHKA ATTRIBUTION: LEARNING TOATTRIBUTE LANGUAGE MODEL OUTPUTS TOREPRESENTATIONS AND WEIGHTS

Aryaman Arora Kirill Acharya Noah Goodman Dan Jurafsky Stanford University aryamana@stanford.edu

Nathan Hu Yanzhe Zhang Christopher Potts

## ABSTRACT

Attributing language model outputs to their internal computations is an open problem in interpretability. Existing methods, which use causal interventions, gradients, or learnable masks, either are infeasibly expensive or struggle to identify actual causally-important internal computations. We propose framing attribution as the problem of identifying nested subsets of internal components which minimise a downstream loss. To learn this task, we introduce Matryoshka Attribution (MAttr), a mask learning method that parametrises the mask with a simple differentiable sigmoid top-k operator. We supervise training over all sparsities simultaneously by randomising k over training, resulting in a learned ordering of components by attribution score. MAttr achieves number 1 on the official leaderboard of the Mechanistic Interpretability Benchmark (Mueller et al., 2025); our method identifies sparse and task-transferrable circuits across varying circuit bases. As a practical application, we show that MAttr can be trained with reinforcement learning to identify weight changes responsible for downstream behaviours in LLM finetuning. We train MAttr on refusal judge scores and find that restoring 1% of Llama 3.1 8B Instruct’s weights to their base model state is sufficient to remove refusals while maintaining capabilities. We view MAttr as a successful formulation of interpretability into a learnable objective that we can tackle with gradient descent, and encourage future work along these lines.

aryamanarora/matryoshka-attribution

## 1 INTRODUCTION

Language models perform substantial internal computation which is never verbalised in humanunderstandable terms (Turpin et al., 2023; Lindsey et al., 2025; Chen et al., 2025). For auditing model alignment (Marks et al., 2025b; Zhong et al., 2026), reliably editing and improving models (Wu et al., 2024a), and especially for our scientific curiosity, we seek to understand these computations. Particularly, we want to know which internal computations caused a model output and what functional role individual model components play (Sharkey et al., 2025). To attribute network outputs to their internals, we have a toolbox of methods which trade off performance and reliability:

1. Causal interventions, which substitute each component’s activation and observe causal effects on model output, at the cost of a separate forward pass per variable;

2. Gradient-based attribution methods, which are parallelisable over all variables via a single backward pass, but practically may fail to identify causally effective computations;

3. Mask learning methods, which are parallelisable and benefit from gradient descent but require significant manual hyperparameter tuning and suffer from training instability.

Given these tradeoffs, no one attribution method is clearly best for all interpretability usecases. An ideal method would combine all their virtues: causality, parallelisability, and simplicity.

We propose Matryoshka Attribution (MAttr), a mask-learning method with two key innovations:

1. Learning masks over varying sparsities jointly by using a randomly-sampled budget k for the mask at every train step, i.e. the Matryoshka technique (Kusupati et al., 2022);

![](images/425ed3798c85b182c3f9c0b9681bcbe63de8b8086cccb750a8773eb54bb51b1d.jpg)  
Figure 1: Matryoshka Attribution: One step of algorithm 1.

2. Parametrising the mask with the differentiable sigmoid top-k function (Wijk et al., 2025).

Unlike prior mask-learning methods, MAttr discards the explicit sparsity loss term (usually an inexact surrogate for L0) and requires no approximations for differentiating through hard masks (e.g. straightthrough estimators, the Gumbel trick). Figure 1 provides an overview of the method. We further explain our approach and how it relates to alternative attribution methods in §3.

We show that MAttr is a highly effective technique for localising causally-important computations in language models. MAttr sets state-of-the-art performance on the Mechanistic Interpretability Benchmark, with an official secret test-set score of 5.6 vs. 1.95 for the runner-up, at lower cost than baselines and achieves consistently strong performance across evaluation metrics and circuit granularities (§4). The attributions learned by MAttr transfer across related benchmarks, serving as evidence in favour of generalisation. We then turn to parameter-level attribution for understanding weight changes over training (§5). We show that MAttr can be trained with reinforcement learning to attribute qualitative LLM behaviours to parameter changes over a finetuning process. We demonstrate that restoring 1% of the parameters of Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct to their base model state is sufficient to remove refusals while maintaining capabilities, at comparable performance to baselines that are free to modify all parameters.

## 2 RELATED WORK

Causal interventions for attribution. The theory of causal abstraction (Geiger et al., 2020; 2021; 2025) along with concurrent approaches (Vig et al., 2020; Meng et al., 2022; Chan et al., 2022; Goldowsky-Dill et al., 2023) introduced interchange interventions: for a given neural network component, a counterfactual representation is swapped into a base representation for a given component, and the resulting change in output is used to measure its direct causal effect (Pearl, 2001). In language models, causal interventions have been used to identify task-relevant components and feature geometry (Geiger et al., 2024) for linguistic (Wang et al., 2023; Hanna et al., 2023; Arora et al., 2024; Prakash et al., 2024; Merullo et al., 2024, inter alia) and mathematical behaviours (Conmy et al., 2023; Stolfo et al., 2023; Wu et al., 2023; Nikankin et al., 2025; Feucht et al., 2026), and to steer (Li et al., 2023b; Wu et al., 2024a; Huang et al., 2024) and train models (Geiger et al., 2022). Causal interventions can be infeasibly expensive in practice. First, causal interventions require a single forward pass per component; parallelisation demands inexact approximations (Nanda, 2023). Second, since neural network components need not compose linearly (McGrath et al., 2023), a complete causal account of internals requires interventions on the power set of n components, at the cost of 2<sup>n</sup> forward passes (Sundararajan & Najmi, 2020).

Gradient-based attribution. Early work on saliency maps for neural networks used gradients to estimate attributions to internal computations (Simonyan et al., 2014; Baehrens et al., 2010). Shrikumar et al. (2017) introduced input-times-gradient (I×G), followed by Sundararajan et al. (2017) introducing Integrated Gradients; we discuss these baselines further in §4. Separately, Layerwise Relevance Propagation (LRP; Bach et al., 2015) applies Taylor approximations of nonlinear operators to assign credit through them. These techniques have been adapted to language models (Shrikumar et al., 2018; Dhamdhere et al., 2019; Janizek et al., 2021; Syed et al., 2024; Hanna et al., 2024; Marks et al., 2025a; Jafari et al., 2024; 2025; Arora et al., 2026b;a). While gradient-based attribution is parallelisable over components via backpropagation, it can be an unreliable approximation of causal interventions, and more generally it may not optimise the downstream interpretability metric of interest (Bilodeau et al., 2024).

T; loss metric L; learning rate η; optimiser OptStep.   
Output: scores $\mathbf { S } ^ { * } = \left\{ s _ { H } \right\}$   
1: $\mathbf { \bar { S } }  \mathbf { 0 }$ ▷ learned scores   
2: for $t = 1$ to T do   
3: sample budget $k \sim$ Uniform $( 1 , | \mathbf { S } | - 1 )$ ▷ budget for current step   
4: sample pair with labels $\langle \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , \dot { y } _ { b } , \dot { y } _ { s } \rangle \sim \overset { \cdot } { D }$   
5: compute c<sub>k</sub> via bisection ▷ definition 2   
6: $\pmb { \alpha } ^ { ( k ) }  \sigma ( \mathbf { S } + c _ { k } )$ ▷ sigmoid top-k mask; $\textstyle \sum \alpha ^ { ( k ) } = k$   
7: $\hat { y } \gets \mathcal { M } _ { \bf H  H ^ { * } } ( { \bf b } , { \bf s } , \alpha ^ { ( k ) } )$ ▷ soft intervention on non-top-k variables

10: return S

Mask learning. A third approach learns to mask out unimportant parts of a neural network while maintaining performance on a task of interest, via gradient descent on the task loss along with a sparsity loss. Many mask learning approaches adopt the hard concrete parametrisation of a mask proposed in Louizos et al. (2018). Mask learning has been used to interpret (De Cao et al., 2020; Cao et al., 2021; Csordás et al., 2021; Lepori et al., 2023; Davies et al., 2023; Prakash et al., 2024; Wu et al., 2023; Bhaskar et al., 2024) and prune language models (Voita et al., 2019; Michel et al., 2019; Sanh et al., 2020; Guo et al., 2021; Panigrahi et al., 2023). Mask learning can be expensive and unstable. First, the additional sparsity loss term and the use of gradient estimators means that mask learning poses significant hyperparameter tuning and training stability problems (Gale et al., 2019; Yin et al., 2019; Bhaskar et al., 2024). Second, sweeping sparsities requires training additional masks, whereas alternative approaches produce an attribution ordering which one can sweep sparsities on for free at evaluation time.

## 3 MATRYOSHKA ATTRIBUTION

Given a model and a set of internal variables, we seek to learn attribution scores which tell us the order in which we should add variables to the unintervened set in order to minimise a given loss.

We refer to causal abstraction (Geiger et al., 2025): a model M is a directed acyclic graph over variables V, where each internal variable H is computed from its parents $\operatorname { P a } ( H )$ by a mechanism $\mathcal { F } _ { H } . \mathcal { M } ( \mathbf { x } )$ refers to the output of the model under input x, with all variables evaluated in topological order via their corresponding mechanism; $h ( \mathbf { x } )$ indicates the value of H in that process. We term the set of variables we attribute over (e.g. MLP neurons, attention heads, etc.) the basis $\mathbf { H } \subseteq \mathbf { V }$

Now, we define soft interchange intervention on the computations of variables in the model, subject to a mask weight α. We replace the mechanisms $\mathcal { F } _ { H }$ and evaluate in topological order as before:

Definition 1 (Soft interchange intervention). Given a base input b, source input s and a mask weight $\alpha _ { H } \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ , a soft interchange intervention on variable H replaces its mechanism $\mathcal { F } _ { H }$ with

$$
\mathcal { F } _ { H } ^ { * } ( \mathbf { u } ) = \alpha _ { H } \mathcal { F } _ { H } ( \mathbf { u } ) + \left( 1 - \alpha _ { H } \right) h ( \mathbf { s } )\tag{1}
$$

where u is the setting ofthe parents ofH in the intervened model and $h ( \mathbf { s } )$ is the value ofH on the unintervened source run. For leafnodes (inputs), we replace $\mathcal { F } _ { H } ( \mathbf { u } )$ with h(b).

The output of the model under soft interchange intervention of the set of variables H is notated a $\mathcal { M } _ { \mathbf { H }  \mathbf { H } ^ { * } } ( \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , \alpha )$ , where b and s are the base and source inputs, and α is a mask over variables.

We present MAttr in algorithm 1. We initialise our attribution scores $\mathbf { S } = \mathbf { 0 }$ . At each step, we sample a budget $k \sim \mathrm { U n i f o r m } \mathbf { \bar { ( } } 1 , | \mathbf { S } | - 1 )$ and a datapoint $d \sim \mathcal { D }$ consisting of inputs b, s and labels $y _ { b } , y _ { s }$ We use the budget k and scores S to parametrise a sigmoid top-k mask over the variables:

Definition 2 (Sigmoid top-k mask; Wijk et al., 2025). Given S and k as above sigmoid top-k is:

$$
\mathsf { S i g T o p K } _ { k } ( \mathbf { S } ) = \sigma ( \mathbf { S } + c _ { k } )\tag{2}
$$

where $c _ { k } \in \mathbb { R }$ solves $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { H } \mathsf { S i g T o p K } _ { k } ( s _ { H } ) = k } \end{array}$ via bisection.

We set $\alpha _ { H } = { \sf S i g T o p K } _ { k } ( s _ { H } )$ as an argument to soft interchange interventions (definition 1) on each of the variables H, i.e. intervening on non-top-k variables. Given a loss function $\mathcal { L } ( \hat { y } , y )$ which compares the intervened prediction yˆ and a label $y ,$ , we compute loss with respect to the base label $y _ { b }$ Finally, we update the scores S by backpropagating from the loss term ℓ.

In expectation, MAttr minimises the loss over varying sparsities:

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } _ { \mathbf { S } } \{ \mathbb { E } _ { k \sim p ( k ) } \big [ \mathcal { L } \big ( \mathcal { M } _ { \mathbf { H }  \mathbf { H } ^ { * } } \big ( \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , \sigma \big ( \mathbf { S } + c _ { k } \big ) \big ) , y _ { b } , y _ { s } \big ) \big ] \}\tag{3}
$$

Since our mask given budget k is parametrised using a soft top-k operator, the mask values for a given variable monotonically increase with k, going from intervened to unintervened. Because of this nesting property, we term the algorithm Matryoshka attribution.<sup>1</sup>

Connection to gradient-based attribution. Although MAttr is a mask-learning method, if we limit its training it recovers two gradient-based attribution techniques. First, the first-step gradient of the loss ℓ with respect to the scores S is equivalent to centred $\mathbf { I } { \times } \mathbf { G }$ (Shrikumar et al., 2017) evaluated at the masked forward. Second, in expectation, after one step of training under SGD, the scores are equivalent to centred Integrated Gradients (Sundararajan et al., 2017), with the path weighted by the budget distribution $p ( k )$ . We provide proofs in $\ S \mathrm { A }$

It is worth pointing out that the “impossibility theorem” of Bilodeau et al. (2024) about Integrated Gradients does not apply to MAttr. They show that any attribution method that is both complete and linear can fail to beat random guessing when it comes to inferring a model’s local counterfactual behavior. First, MAttr learns an attribution ordering that is insensitive to constant shifts in the scores, so it is not constrained by these attribution axioms (see §A.4). Second, and much more substantively, MAttr is actually an instance of what Bilodeau et al. (2024) call for in response to their result: $\mathbf { \ddot { a } }$ method that directly optimizes the task” by allowing the practitioner to define a loss function, rather than defining an axiomatically determined outcome.

## 4 ATTRIBUTION TO REPRESENTATIONS

We train and evaluate MAttr on the Circuit Localization track of MIB (Mueller et al., 2025) as well as additional variants and tasks that we collect. As of this writing, edge-level MAttr is number 1 on the official MIB leaderboard (which uses a secret test set; in this paper we only report public test set scores),<sup>2</sup> with an average score across models and benchmarks of 5.6 vs. 1.95 for the number 2 entrant and percentage increases ranging from 45.9% to 343.0% over the runner-up for each model–benchmark combination.

Benchmark setup. For MIB, each dataset D has samples $\langle \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , y _ { b } , y _ { s } \rangle$ , where b and s are base and counterfactual inputs and $y _ { b }$ and $y _ { s }$ are corresponding next-token labels. Given predicted logits yˆ, the faithfulness metric is the normalised logit difference when intervening on the non-top-k variables by attribution score. Let $\beta ^ { ( k ) }$ be a hard mask on non-top-k variables:

$$
\hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } = \mathcal { M } _ { \mathrm { H }  \mathrm { H } ^ { \ast } } ( \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , \beta ^ { ( k ) } ) ; \qquad \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } , y ) = [ \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } ] _ { y _ { b } } - [ \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } ] _ { y _ { s } }\tag{4}
$$

$$
\mathsf { F a i t h } ( k ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \langle \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , y _ { b } , y _ { s } \rangle \sim \mathcal { D } } \left[ \frac { \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } , y ) - \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( 0 ) } , y ) } { \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( \vert \mathbf { H } \vert ) } , y ) - \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( 0 ) } , y ) } \right]\tag{5}
$$

The main evaluation metric, CPR, is defined as the area under the faithfulness curve over proportions $\mathbf { P } = \{ . 0 0 1 , . 0 0 2 , . 0 0 5 , . 0 1 , . 0 2 , . 0 5 , . 1 , . 2 , . 5 , 1 \}$ , via the trapezoidal rule with $k _ { i } = \mathsf { \lfloor p _ { i } \lvert { H } \rvert } \mathsf { \dot { \Omega } } ^ { \bullet } { \mathsf { C P R } } =$ $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m - 1 } \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( p _ { i + 1 } - p _ { i } \right) \left( \mathsf { F a i t h } ( k _ { i } ) + \mathsf { F a i t h } ( k _ { i + 1 } ) \right) } \end{array}$ . Finally, MIB originally considers as bases node (each attention head, MLP block, and the input) and edge (each residual term corresponding to an interaction between nodes) as bases. To this, we add mlp (each MLP neuron and token), mlp+attn (each MLP neuron and attention head at each token), sae (each MLP output SAE feature and token).

Models and tasks. In total, MIB has 12 task–model pairs. MIB studies 4 models: gpt2-small, Qwen2.5-0.5B, gemma-2-2b, and Llama-3.1-8B (all base models). The tasks are:

• ioi: Indirect Object Identification (Wang et al., 2023, for all 4 models)

• arithmetic: a templatic addition and a subtraction task (Stolfo et al., 2023, only Llama)

• mcqa: a multiple-choice question-answer task (Wiegreffe et al., 2025, all except GPT-2)

• arc-e (for Llama and Gemma), arc-c (Clark et al., 2018, for Llama)

We extend the benchmark and term it MIB+: for the three new bases we introduce (MLP neuron, MLP+Attn, SAE), we attribute per-token, and we experiment with 4 subject-verb agreement tasks from Marks et al. (2025a), and 4 arithmetic tasks from Feucht et al. (2026). We only use Llama for these settings, and we use Llama Scope (He et al., 2024) for the MLP output SAEs. We treat the SAE error term as a leaf node (interpolated between constants) that receives its own attribution score.

Methods. Rather than reusing MIB results, we tune and re-evaluate I×G (Shrikumar et al., 2017) and Integrated Gradients (Sundararajan et al., 2017). We also add Interchange Interventions (Geiger et al., 2025), Expected Gradients (Erion et al., 2021), GIM (Edin et al., 2026), AttnLRP (Achtibat et al., 2024), DBM (Wu et al., 2024b), and Node Pruning (Bhaskar et al., 2024). We report hyperparameters in §B.1. Briefly, we describe the baselines; details are in §C. First, Interchange Interventions merely patch the activation of an individual variable with its source state h(s) and measure the causal effect on the modified forward pass to compute its attribution score. Now, for gradient-based methods we use $\ell = \mathcal { L } _ { \sf M I B }$ . I×G is a Taylor approximation of feature attribution using the local gradient at b and the delta between b and s:

$$
s _ { H } ^ { \mathrm { l x G } } = ( h ( \mathbf { b } ) - h ( \mathbf { s } ) ) \frac { \partial \ell ( \mathbf { x } ) } { \partial H } \Big | _ { \mathbf { x } = \mathbf { b } }\tag{6}
$$

AttnLRP, GIM, and RelP modify I×G by applying various manual modifications of backpropagation operators for Transformer components. Integrated Gradients (IG) extends this by numerically

Figure 4: Further analysis on MIB and additional node-level experiments with MAttr.

integrating the gradient over the straight line path between pairs via a Riemann sum with m steps:

$$
\mathbf { x } ^ { ( i ) } = \frac { i } { m } \mathbf { b } + \left( 1 - \frac { i } { m } \right) \mathbf { s } , \qquad s _ { H } ^ { \scriptscriptstyle { [ 6 ] } } = \left( h ( \mathbf { b } ) - h ( \mathbf { s } ) \right) \frac { 1 } { m } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { \partial \ell ( \mathbf { x } ) } { \partial H } \Big | _ { \mathbf { x } = \mathbf { x } ^ { ( i ) } }\tag{7}
$$

Expected Gradients replaces the Riemann sum with the gradient at a randomly sampled point on the straight line path, computing the same quantity in expectation.

Our two mask learning baselines are DBM and Node Pruning. We use a simple version of DBM which uses a sigmoid mask with a temperature parameter annealed over training and an L1 penalty on post-mask scores. For Node Pruning, we learn masks as a function of latent scores through the hard concrete distribution, with a sparsity loss based on the mask L0. For both methods we train masks at varying sparsities; for the ‘sweep’ evaluations, at each MIB evaluation point, we use the sparsity level whose L0 is within the budget. This costs ≈ 50× as much as MAttr; see §E.2.

We use logit difference $\mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } , y )$ as the attribution target for gradient baselines, and maximise it for trained methods. We report training hyperparameters for all methods in §B.1; we adhere to MIB’s original budgets for gradient-based methods (cost in Figure 2, bottom), and standardise methods to 5000 train steps for MIB+. For training MAttr with Adam, we find that for node-level only LR needs tuning (and a large range of settings succeed), but for more granular bases we additionally set $\epsilon = 1 0 ^ { - 2 } ;$ theory in §A.3 shows that small-epsilon Adam ignores gradient magnitude, which can hurt data efficiency. We use the exact per-task evaluation settings provided in the MIB codebase.

Result 1: MAttr sets state-of-the-art performance on MIB and MIB+. We report results on the test set for both node- and edge-level circuit locations in Figure 2. On the test set of both nodeand edge-level circuit localisation, MAttr statistically significantly outperforms all tested baselines $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ . We use a two-sided paired Wilcoxon signed-rank test with the Holm–Bonferroni method over each of the MIB subtasks and each baseline (§E.3).

Result 2: MAttr optimises both circuit performance and sparsity across bases. Theoretically, CPR has two flaws as a metric: (1) logit difference is unbounded, so faithfulness can become far larger than 1 at certain sparsities and average out faithfulness below 1 at other sparsities; (2) the CPR evaluation grid measures linear area despite using a log scale, so the score is dominated by the large circuit regime.<sup>3</sup> As a result, CPR may fail to reward high performance at small circuit sizes. We therefore introduce compactness, a metric based on interchange intervention accuracy:

$$
1 1 \mathsf { A } ( k ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \langle b , s , y _ { b } , y _ { s } \rangle \sim \mathcal { D } } \left[ \mathbb { 1 } \left[ [ \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } ] _ { y _ { b } } > [ \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } ] _ { y _ { s } } \right] \right]\tag{8}
$$

$$
\mathsf { C o m p a c t n e s s } ( \mathbf { k } ) = \left( \log k _ { n } - \log k _ { 1 } \right) ^ { - 1 } \sum _ { i } \left( \log k _ { i + 1 } - \log k _ { i } \right) \frac { \mathsf { I I A } ( k _ { i } ) + \mathsf { I I A } ( k _ { i + 1 } ) } { 2 }\tag{9}
$$

Algorithm 2 MATTR for parameters   
Input: model $\mathcal { M } _ { \theta } ,$ parameter groups $\theta ;$ base weights $\theta _ { b } ;$ counterfactual weights $\theta _ { s } ;$ dataset D of prompts $\langle \mathbf { x } \rangle$   
steps $T ;$ reward function $r _ { \phi } ;$ prompts per step $B ;$ group size G; learning rate η; optimiser OptStep.   
Output: scores $\mathbf { S } ^ { * } = \left\{ s _ { H } \right\}$   
1: $\mathbf { \bar { S } }  \mathbf { 0 }$ ▷ learned scores   
2: for $t = 1$ to T do   
3: sample budget $k \sim$ Uniform(1, |S| − 1), prompts $\left\{ \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } , \dotsc , \mathbf { x } _ { B } \right\} \sim \mathcal { D }$   
4: compute $c _ { k }$ via bisection ▷ definition 2   
5: prepare parameter mask $\boldsymbol \alpha ^ { ( k ) } \gets \boldsymbol \sigma ( \boldsymbol \mathbf { S } + \boldsymbol c _ { k } )$ $\textstyle > \sum \alpha ^ { ( k ) } = k$   
6: prepare weights $\pmb { \theta } ^ { * } \gets \pmb { \theta } _ { b } + \pmb { \alpha } ^ { ( k ) } \odot \big ( \pmb { \theta } _ { s } - \pmb { \theta } _ { b } \big )$ ▷ soft interchange intervention   
7: for $b = 1$ to B do   
8: sample group of completions $\{ \mathbf { y } _ { b , 1 } , \dots , \mathbf { y } _ { b , G } \} \sim \mathcal { M } _ { \theta  \theta ^ { * } } ( \mathbf { x } _ { b } )$   
9: rewards $r _ { b , i } \gets r _ { \phi } ( \mathbf { x } _ { b } , \mathbf { y } _ { b , i } ) ;$ advantages $A _ { b , i }  ( r _ { b , i } - \overrightarrow { \mathbf { r } _ { b } } ) / ( \overrightarrow { \mathrm { s t d } ( \mathbf { r } _ { b } ) + \epsilon } )$   
10: compute length-normalised losses $\begin{array} { r } { \widehat { \ell } _ { b , i } \gets ( 1 / | \mathbf { y } _ { b , i } | ) \sum _ { t } \log \mathcal { M } _ { \theta  \theta ^ { * } } ( y _ { b , i , t } \mid \mathbf { x } _ { b } , \mathbf { y } _ { b , i , < t } ) } \end{array}$   
11: grab informative groups $\mathcal { T }  \{ b : \mathrm { s t d } ( \mathbf { r } _ { b } ) \geq \dot { \epsilon } \}$   
12: $\begin{array} { r } { \ell \gets - ( 1 / | \mathcal { T } | G ) \sum _ { b \in \mathcal { T } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { G } A _ { b , i } \hat { \ell } _ { b , i } } \end{array}$ ▷ policy gradient   
13: $\mathbf { S } \gets \mathrm { O p t S t e p } ( \mathbf { S } , \eta , \bar { \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial \mathbf { S } } } )$ ▷ gradient reaches S through $\alpha ^ { ( k ) }$   
14: return S

This metric rewards finding a small performance-preserving circuit. We plot CPR against compactness in Figure 3, and we find that MAttr optimises both metrics considerably; at all bases, it has the highest CPR, and on compactness it outperforms or is close behind IG depending on the basis. Furthermore, if one is willing to trade off CPR for higher compactness than IG, we show that using a log-uniform sampling distribution for the budget k enables MAttr to beat IG on compactness.

Result 3: Mask learning sparsity sweeps still underperform MAttr. DBM and Node Pruning at a fixed sparsity over-optimise CPR while having lower compactness than even I×G. Sparsity sweeps reveal that increasing the sparsity coefficient improves compactness but harms CPR (§E.1). The multi-sparsity sweep aggregation helps both metrics but not enough to match MAttr. Notably, both DBM and Node Pruning find nearly nested sets of variables across sparsities (§E.2).

MLP blocks drive disagreement on MIB. We compare the component rankings of MAttr and baselines on node-level MIB tasks in Figure 4a. We find clear disagreement between MAttr and poorly-performing gradient-based methods on MLP blocks but not attention heads. Strong baselines (IntInv, EG, AttnLRP, and mask methods) cluster with MAttr.

MAttr transfers across tasks and recovers known circuits. We use the MAttr ranking for a source task and evaluate it on a target task, for all pairs of tasks on node-level Llama. Figure 4b shows a selection of tasks with strong transfer: e.g. the subtraction task from MIB and the addition and months tasks from Feucht et al. (2026) highly rank heads a15.h13 and a16.h21 (corroborating Nikankin et al., 2025).<sup>4</sup> Figure 4c shows that the Pearson r of cross-task attribution scores correlates with transfer success.

## 5 ATTRIBUTION TO PARAMETERS VIA REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

In the previous section, we attributed model outputs to internal activations using a dataset of paired inputs. Now, to show the generality of MAttr, we change the setting in two independent ways: we will attribute model outputs to (1) model parameters, via a pair of checkpoints, and (2) use reinforcement learning to optimise for non-differentiable targets. This enables localising arbitrary behavioural changes between two model checkpoints to their parameter differences.

MAttr over parameters. In algorithm 1, we used learned scores $\mathbf { S }$ to parametrise soft interventions $h ^ { \ast } ( \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , \alpha )$ on the values of model-internal variables H, which interpolate between paired inputs $\mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s }$ . We now propose interpolating between pairs in weight space rather than input space. Consider a model $\mathcal { M } _ { \theta }$ with parameters from two checkpoints $\theta _ { b } , \theta _ { s }$ . Given a mask vector $\boldsymbol { \alpha } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { \theta }$ , a soft interchange intervention between these checkpoints is:

$$
\theta ^ { \ast } ( \theta _ { b } , \theta _ { s } , \alpha ) = \theta _ { b } + \alpha \odot \left( \theta _ { s } - \theta _ { b } \right)\tag{10}
$$

The model under parameter-level soft interchange intervention is notated as $\mathcal { M } _ { \theta  \theta ^ { * } }$ , and the learned scores S and per-step budget k parameterise the mask $\pmb { \alpha } ^ { ( k ) }$ exactly as in algorithm 1.

Reinforcement learning for MAttr. We now adapt MAttr for training with the Group Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO) loss function (Shao et al., 2024). We assume access to a reward function $r _ { \phi }$ which can score model outputs conditioned on an input. We do not clip updates. We outline the algorithm in algorithm 2; briefly, at each step we prepare intervened weights based on the current budget k, and use this policy to sample groups of completions for each prompt in the batch. We score each completion with the reward function, compute group-normalised advantages, which we average into length-normalised losses that backpropagate into the attribution scores.

For evaluating the resulting attributions, we use a hard mask $\beta ^ { ( k ) }$ and ‘graft’ (following the terminology of Panigrahi et al., 2023; also Feng et al., 2025) the top-k part of the weight delta.

## 5.1 ABLATING REFUSAL

Open-weights instruction-tuned models are trained to refuse harmful requests, whereas corresponding base models do not refuse but generally struggle with instruction following. We show that MAttr can be used to identify the portion of the weight delta between a base and finetuned model that is responsible for refusal but not for general capabilities.

We study refusal in order to demonstrate: (1) MAttr does separate a behaviour of interest from unrelated capabilities without access to held-out data; (2) interpretability techniques can be valuable for auditing the tamper-resistance of open-weights models (Tamirisa et al., 2025); uniquely, our results reveal the risks of releasing un-safeguarded base models alongside safety-tuned checkpoints.

Models, methods, and reward function. We experiment with Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. For MAttr we use uniform-k and attribute instruct vs. base checkpoints of the same model, with the basis being the $d _ { \mathrm { m o d e l } }$ -shape rows of each weight matrix (e.g. each gate, up, and down-projection vector corresponding to a single MLP neuron), and the layernorm vectors.<sup>5</sup> We compare with the following baselines; the first four are standard refusal-removal techniques which are allowed to arbitrarily update model weights; EG is an attribution technique:

• GRPO: No-clip and no-KL GRPO (Shao et al., 2024);

• GRPO+KL: No-clip GRPO with a tuned KL penalty of $\beta = 0 . 0 1$ ;

• Abliteration: Weight orthogonalisation of the residual-stream refusal steering vector from AdvBench (Zou et al., 2023) and Alpaca (Taori et al., 2023) prompts (Arditi et al., 2024);

• GRP-Oblit: Positive-adv.-only GRPO, $\beta = 0 . 0 1$ , cosine decay (Russinovich et al., 2026);

• EG: Expected Gradients on the same loss function as MAttr; attribution baseline.

• Base (URIAL): the original base model with the URIAL helpful-only chat template inst\_1k\_v4.help (Lin et al., 2024) to elicit assistant-like responses from the base model.

We use full-parameter training for GRP-Oblit on the 1B model. For GRPO, GRPO+KL, and 8B GRP-Oblit, we train with LoRA, $r = 3 2 , \alpha = 6 4$ , with a learning rate of $1 0 ^ { - 5 }$

We train GRPO(+KL), GRP-Oblit, and MAttr with the StrongREJECT prompts (Souly et al., 2024), using the default chat template of the model being trained. We evaluate on the small subset of StrongREJECT and train on the remaining held-out prompts. To compute rewards for completions, we use the corresponding StrongReject scorer,<sup>6</sup> a finetuned LLM judge based on gemma-2b that returns helpfulness scores as a probability distribution over integers in [1, 5].

Result: MAttr removes refusal and maintains capabilities with 1–2% of parameters. In Figure 5a, we report downstream harmfulness evaluations on StrongREJECT and (held-out) SORRY-Bench (Xie et al., 2025), and capabilities evaluations on GSM8K, IFEval, and MMLU. We also report the percentage of parameters modified by each method $( \lVert \Delta \theta \rVert _ { 0 } )$ . For both models, the baseline techniques all modify a large portion of parameters $( > 2 7 . 6 \% )$ . MAttr meanwhile matches their performance while restoring only 2% of the parameters of the 1B model and 1% of 8B to their base states. This shows that MAttr can identify a truly causal and localised portion of the weight delta specific to refusal. EG, on the other hand, fails to maintain non-refusal capabilities.

Figure 5b shows how the downstream performance of the model on GSM8K and StrongREJECT changes as we sweep $\| \Delta \theta \| _ { 0 }$ for the MAttr-scored parameter updates. We see that a large range of parameters can maintain GSM8K scores, up until a performance cliff. We show analysis of where the selected weights are along with example generations in §I.

## 6 DISCUSSION

Gradient descent for interpretability research. So far, interpretability research has been slow in the adoption of deep learning for its goals. We view MAttr as one method among a broader trend in this regard; for example, recent research trains metamodels for converting model representations and weights into natural language for auditing (Karvonen et al., 2026; Huang et al., 2025; Fraser-Taliente et al., 2026, inter alia). Like metamodels, MAttr changes interpretability into the problem of specifying an appropriate training objective that can be applied to any (backpropagation-compatible) model and task, rather than merely making interpretability claims on a single setting using bespoke methodology. Our main difference from existing metamodels is that we propose a mechanisticallygrounded training objective using the framework of causal abstraction. Broadly, we advocate for greater and more creative use of deep learning for understanding neural networks.

Reconciling gradient-based attribution, mask learning, and causal interventions. An initial motivation for this work was making sense of the disparate approaches to attribution in neural network interpretability research. We find it satisfying that MAttr, ostensibly a mask-learning technique, has a clear mathematical relationship to Integrated Gradients via its backward operator $( \ S \mathrm { A } . 2 ) ;$ at the same time, it can learn to escape its limitations (Bilodeau et al., 2024). We hope that this work encourages more reflection on and understanding of the profusion of attribution methods.

Future work. Several future directions for MAttr are apparent. First, in this paper, we constrained ourselves to learning scalar attribution scores S over a dataset. More interesting would be training a featuriser f that learns to convert model representations or prompt features into example-, checkpoint-, or task-specific attribution scores, i.e. an attribution metamodel. This idea is similar to the causal importance function of Bushnaq et al. (2025). Second, one could cotrain a model or featuriser along with its scalar attribution scores (similar to optimal ablation; Li & Janson, 2024), essentially using MAttr as a substitute for the L0 penalty. One may also apply MAttr to SAE or DAS training.

## 7 CONCLUSION

We introduced MAttr, a novel method for localising model outputs and open-ended behaviours to changes in representations or weights. Through a variety of benchmarks and case studies, we demonstrated that MAttr is the state-of-the-art attribution method, and we presented new results on task circuitry and cross-task circuit transfer and parameter-level attribution applied to remove refusal from instruction-tuned language models. Furthermore, we supply extensive appendices with analysis of our above results along with additional experiments on emergent misalignment (§J), interference weights in toy models (§K), and Vision Transformers (§L). We look forward to continued applications of deep learning for language model and neural network interpretability.

## A GRADIENT DERIVATION FOR MAttr

For a given loss ℓ, we seek to derive the expression for the gradient to the scores $\frac { \partial \ell } { \partial s _ { H } }$ . Per Wijk et al. (2025), the Jacobian of the sigmoid top-k mask is:

$$
\frac { \partial \mathsf { S i g } \mathsf { T o p K } } { \partial \mathbf { S } } = \frac { \partial \mathsf { S i g } \mathsf { T o p K } } { \partial \mathbf { S } } + \frac { \partial \mathsf { S i g } \mathsf { T o p K } } { \partial c _ { k } } \frac { \partial c _ { k } } { \partial \mathbf { S } } = \mathrm { d i a g } ( \sigma ^ { \prime } ( \mathbf { S } + c _ { k } ) ) - \frac { \sigma ^ { \prime } ( \mathbf { S } + c _ { k } ) \sigma ^ { \prime } ( \mathbf { S } + c _ { k } ) ^ { \top } } { \sum _ { H } \sigma ^ { \prime } \left( s _ { H } + c _ { k } \right) }\tag{11}
$$

where $\sigma ^ { \prime } ( x ) = \sigma ( x ) ( 1 - \sigma ( x ) )$ . Wijk et al. also provide an implicit differentiation derivation for $k ,$ but we detach k from the graph in all our experiments since we pass it in manually.

## A.1 MAttr IS ONLINE MASKED I×G

Throughout, $\alpha _ { H } = { \sf S i g T o p K } _ { k } ( s _ { H } )$ is the keep weight of definition 1: $\alpha _ { H } = 1$ leaves H at its base-run computation and $\alpha _ { H } = 0$ replaces it with its source value, so the budget $\begin{array} { r } { k = \sum _ { H } \alpha _ { H } } \end{array}$ counts kept variables. Let $\sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } : = \sigma ^ { \prime } ( s _ { H } + c _ { k } )$ and let $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } : = \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial h _ { H } ^ { * } } \frac { \partial h _ { H } ^ { * } } { \partial \alpha _ { H } } = \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial h _ { H } ^ { * } } \big ( \mathcal { F } _ { H } ( \mathbf { u } ) - h ( \mathbf { s } ) \big ) } \end{array}$ be the I×G effect of node H (the same base-minus-source direction as the I×G baseline of §4), where by definition 1 both factors are evaluated at the masked forward pass: $\partial \ell / \partial h _ { H } ^ { * }$ is the gradient through the intervened model, and the delta is taken from the value $\mathcal { F } _ { H } ( \mathbf { u } )$ that H computes from its (possibly intervened) parents, not from the clean base run h(b). We notate $\delta _ { i j }$ for the indicator variable $\mathbb { 1 } [ i = j ]$ . Since α = SigTopK(S),

$$
\frac { \partial \ell } { \partial s _ { H } } = \sum _ { j } \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial h _ { j } ^ { * } } \frac { \partial h _ { j } ^ { * } } { \partial \alpha _ { j } } \frac { \partial \alpha _ { j } } { \partial s _ { H } } = \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } \frac { \partial \mathsf { S i g T o p K } _ { j } } { \partial s _ { H } }\tag{12}
$$

$$
= \sum _ { j } { \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } \left( \delta _ { j H } \sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } - { \frac { \sigma _ { j } ^ { \prime } \sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } } { \sum _ { i } { \sigma _ { i } ^ { \prime } } } } \right) }\tag{13}
$$

$$
= \sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } \left( \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } - \frac { \sum _ { j } \sigma _ { j } ^ { \prime } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } } { \sum _ { i } \sigma _ { i } ^ { \prime } } \right)\tag{14}
$$

$$
\mathbf { \Sigma } = \sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } \left( \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } - \mathbf { \overline { { \mathrm { I x G } } } } \right) , \qquad \mathbf { \overline { { \mathrm { I x G } } } } \equiv \frac { \sum _ { j } \sigma _ { j } ^ { \prime } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } } { \sum _ { j } \sigma _ { j } ^ { \prime } }\tag{15}
$$

We can interpret the gradient of sigmoid top-k as applying a soft mask to the I×G effect $\mathrm { I x G } _ { H }$ focusing updates onto scores which are near the current mask boundary $( c _ { k } )$ and centring them by the IxG term.

Note that this gradient is subtracted in updates, $\Delta s _ { H } = - \eta \sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } \big ( \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } - \overline { { \mathrm { I x G } } } \big )$ under SGD, so a variable whose restoration towards its base value lowers the loss by more than the weighted average gains score. We can thus view the learning algorithm as accumulating masked and centred I×G effects (of the negated loss) for the variables of interest.

A major difference from $\mathbf { I } { \times } \mathbf { G }$ is that both the gradient term $\frac { \partial \ell } { \partial h _ { H } ^ { * } }$ and the activation delta $\mathcal { F } _ { H } ( \mathbf { u } ) - h ( \mathbf { s } )$ are computed with variables under varying degrees of soft intervention, not at the fully unintervened forward pass. This is similar to how IG (and its variant operationalisations) average gradients or gradient effects at various degrees of soft intervention.

Identity straight-through estimator. Suppose that instead of directly using sigmoid top-k, we instead use hard top-k for the forward and differentiate through it via an identity straight-through estimator (Bengio et al., 2013), i.e. SigTopK $\mathbf { \rho } _ { \mathrm { i d } } ( \mathbf { S } ) = \mathbf { S }$ . For this estimator, the Jacobian is:

$$
\frac { \partial \mathsf { S i g T o p K } _ { \mathrm { i d } } } { \partial \mathbf { S } } = \mathbf { I }\tag{16}
$$

The gradient derivation is much simpler under this estimator:

$$
\frac { \partial \ell } { \partial s _ { H } } = \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial h } \frac { \partial h } { \partial \pmb { \alpha } } \frac { \partial \pmb { \alpha } } { \partial s _ { H } } = \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } \frac { \partial \mathsf { S i g } \mathsf { T o p } \mathsf { K } _ { \mathrm { i d } , j } } { \partial s _ { H } }\tag{17}
$$

$$
= \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } \delta _ { j H }\tag{18}
$$

$$
= { \mathrm { I x G } } _ { H }\tag{19}
$$

i.e. exactly the I×G effect. Therefore, under the identity straight-through estimator, MAttr simply accumulates (negated) I×G scores under online (non-)top-k interventions.

## A.2 THE FIRST GRADIENT STEP OF MAttr+SGD IS PATH-REWEIGHTED IG

Theorem 1 (Expected MAttr scores after the first update). Let $\ell ( \pmb { \alpha } ) : = \mathscr { L } ( \mathcal { M } _ { \mathbf { H }  \mathbf { H } ^ { * } } ( \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } , \pmb { \alpha } ) , y _ { b } )$ and let $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { I G } _ { H } ^ { \rho } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \rho ( t ) \frac { \partial \ell ( { \pmb \alpha } ) } { \partial { \alpha } _ { H } } \bigg | _ { { \pmb \alpha } = t { 1 } } } \end{array}$ dt be a version ofIG where the path is weighted by thefunction $\rho ( t )$ ; the integrand is exactly $\mathrm { I x G } _ { H }$ evaluated at the uniform mask t1, which we write $\mathrm { \dot { \mathbf { x } } G } _ { H } ( t )$ below; the path runsfrom thefully patched source run $( t = 0 )$ to the clean base run $( t = 1 ) . ^ { 7 }$ Given learning rate $\eta ,$ the scores that result from the first training step of MAttr are, in expectation:

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { k \sim p ( k ) } \left[ s _ { H } ^ { ( 0 ) } - \eta \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial s _ { H } ^ { ( 0 ) } } \right] = - \frac { \eta } { 6 } \Big ( \mathbf { I } \mathbb { G } _ { H } ^ { \rho } - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathbf { I } \mathbb { G } _ { j } ^ { \rho } \Big ) + O ( 1 / N ) , \qquad \rho ( t ) = 6 t ( 1 - t )\tag{20}
$$

Proof. Under the default uniform schedule, $k \sim$ Uniform $( 1 , N - 1 )$ , which we treat as continuous with density $\begin{array} { r } { p ( k ) = \frac { 1 } { N - 1 } } \end{array}$ . At zero initialisation every $\boldsymbol { \sigma } _ { j } ^ { \prime }$ is equal, so $\begin{array} { r } { \overline { { \mathrm { I x G } } } = \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } } \end{array}$

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { k } \left[ \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial s _ { H } } \right] = \int _ { 1 } ^ { N - 1 } p ( k ) \sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } \left( \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } - \overline { { \mathrm { I x G } } } \right) \mathrm { d } k\tag{21}
$$

$$
= \int _ { 1 } ^ { N - 1 } { \frac { 1 } { N - 1 } } \kappa ( 1 - \kappa ) \left( \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } ( \kappa ) - { \frac { 1 } { N } } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } ( \kappa ) \right) \mathrm { d } k \qquad { \mathrm { ( z e r o ~ i n i t , ~ e q u a l ~ s c o r e s ) } }\tag{22}
$$

$$
= \frac { N } { N - 1 } \int _ { 1 / N } ^ { 1 - 1 / N } \kappa ( 1 - \kappa ) \left( \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } ( \kappa ) - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } ( \kappa ) \right) \mathrm { d } \kappa \quad ( \mathrm { s u b . } \kappa = \frac { k } { N } , \mathrm { d } k = N \mathrm { d } \kappa )\tag{23}
$$

$$
= \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t ( 1 - t ) \left( \operatorname { I x } \operatorname { G } _ { H } ( t ) - { \frac { 1 } { N } } \sum _ { j } \operatorname { I x } \mathbf { G } _ { j } ( t ) \right) \mathrm { d } t + O ( 1 / N ) { \mathrm { ~ } } \qquad { \mathrm { ( r e n a m e ~ } } t = \kappa ; { \mathrm { e x t . ~ t o ~ } } [ 0 , 1 ] { \mathrm { ) } }\tag{24}
$$

$$
= \frac { 1 } { 6 } \Big ( \mathrm { I G } _ { H } ^ { \rho } - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I G } _ { j } ^ { \rho } \Big ) + O ( 1 / N ) , \qquad \rho ( t ) = 6 t ( 1 - t )\tag{25}
$$

Since SGD subtracts the gradient, the expected score after the first update is:

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \Delta s _ { H } ] = - \frac { \eta } { 6 } \Big ( \mathrm { I G } _ { H } ^ { \rho } - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I G } _ { j } ^ { \rho } \Big ) + O ( 1 / N )\tag{26}
$$

For the logit-difference objective of §4, which is maximised, $\ell = - \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } }$ and the sign flips back: the scores are a positive multiple of the centred, path-reweighted IG of the logit difference. □

Changing the budget schedule merely reweights the IG path by defining a different $\rho ( t )$ . For example, for the log-uniform schedule $\begin{array} { r } { p ( k ) \stackrel { \cdot } { = } \frac { 1 } { k \ln N } } \end{array}$ we have $\rho ( t ) = 2 ( 1 - t )$ , which up-weights the sparse end of the path near the source run $( t \to \bar { 0 } )$ , and for the logit schedule we can have exactly $\rho ( t ) = 1$

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \Delta s _ { H } ] = - \frac { \eta } { 2 \ln N } \Big ( \mathrm { I G } _ { H } ^ { \rho } - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I G } _ { j } ^ { \rho } \Big ) + O ( 1 / N ) , \qquad \rho ( t ) = 2 ( 1 - t )\tag{27}
$$

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \Delta s _ { H } ] = - \frac { \eta } { 2 \ln N } \Big ( \mathrm { I G } _ { H } - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I G } _ { j } \Big ) + O ( 1 / N )\tag{28}
$$

## A.3 THE FIRST GRADIENT STEP OF MAttr+ADAM DEPENDS ON ϵ

As in §A.2, we derive the first-step expected scores for MAttr when training with Adam. We show that the size of ϵ relative to the gradient magnitudes changes the derivation: namely, large ϵ results in IG-like scores, while small ϵ leads to only gradient signs being relevant (not magnitude), which results in a different type of attribution. We empirically confirm this later in Figure 8.

Let $\begin{array} { r } { d _ { H } ( t ) : = \mathrm { I x G } _ { H } ( t ) - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { i } \mathrm { I x G } _ { j } ( t ) } \end{array}$ be the centred $\mathbf { I } { \times } \mathbf { G }$ effect at the uniform mask t1, as in $\ S \mathrm { A } . 2$ . At zero initialisation, $\sigma _ { H } ^ { \prime } \overset { \cdot } { = } t ( 1 - t )$ for all H with $t = k / N$ the kept fraction, so

$$
g _ { H } ( t ) : = \frac { \partial \ell } { \partial s _ { H } } = t ( 1 - t ) d _ { H } ( t ) .\tag{29}
$$

With bias correction, Adam’s first step has $\hat { m } _ { 1 } = g$ and $\hat { v } _ { 1 } = g ^ { 2 }$ independently of $\beta _ { 1 } , \beta _ { 2 }$ , giving

$$
\Delta s _ { H } = - \eta { \frac { \hat { m } _ { H } } { \sqrt { \hat { v } _ { H } } + \epsilon } } = - \eta { \frac { g _ { H } ( t ) } { | g _ { H } ( t ) | + \epsilon } } = - \eta { \frac { t ( 1 - t ) d _ { H } ( t ) } { t ( 1 - t ) | d _ { H } ( t ) | + \epsilon } } .\tag{30}
$$

Under the uniform schedule, the substitution $t = k / N$ of $\ S \mathrm { A } . 2$ induces the density $\begin{array} { r } { p ( t ) = \frac { N } { N - 1 } } \end{array}$ on $[ 1 / N , 1 - 1 / N ]$ , i.e. the uniform density on [0, 1] up to $O ( 1 / N )$ , so $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \cdot ] = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } ( \cdot ) \mathrm { d } t + O ( 1 / N ) } \end{array}$

Large ϵ. Assume $\epsilon \gg t ( 1 - t ) \left| d _ { H } ( t ) \right|$ | for all H and t. Expanding $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { g } { | g | + \epsilon } = \frac { g } { \epsilon } \big ( 1 - \frac { | g | } { \epsilon } + O ( g ^ { 2 } / \epsilon ^ { 2 } ) \big ) } \end{array}$ in eq. (30),

$$
\Delta s _ { H } = - \frac { \eta } { \epsilon } t ( 1 - t ) d _ { H } ( t ) + \frac { \eta } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } t ^ { 2 } ( 1 - t ) ^ { 2 } d _ { H } ( t ) | d _ { H } ( t ) | + { \cal O } ( \eta \epsilon ^ { - 3 } ) .\tag{31}
$$

Taking the expectation over t,

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \Delta s _ { H } ] = - \frac { \eta } { \epsilon } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t ( 1 - t ) d _ { H } ( t ) \mathrm { d } t + \frac { \eta } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t ^ { 2 } ( 1 - t ) ^ { 2 } d _ { H } ( t ) \left| d _ { H } ( t ) \right| \mathrm { d } t + O ( 1 / N ) + O ( \eta \epsilon ^ { - 3 } )\tag{32}
$$

$$
= - \frac { \eta } { 6 \epsilon } \Bigl ( \mathrm { I G } _ { H } ^ { \rho } - \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j } \mathrm { I G } _ { j } ^ { \rho } \Bigr ) + \frac { \eta } { 3 0 \epsilon ^ { 2 } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \rho _ { 2 } ( t ) d _ { H } ( t ) | d _ { H } ( t ) | \mathrm { d } t + O ( 1 / N ) + O ( \eta \epsilon ^ { - 3 } ) ,\tag{33}
$$

where $\rho ( t ) = 6 t ( 1 - t )$ is the $\mathtt { B e t a } ( 2 , 2 )$ path weight of $\ S \mathrm { A } . 2$ and $\rho _ { 2 } ( t ) = 3 0 t ^ { 2 } ( 1 - t ) ^ { 2 }$ is the Beta(3, 3) density. To leading order in $1 / \epsilon$ , large-ϵ Adam recovers the path-reweighted IG of $\ S \mathrm { A } . 2$ with effective learning rate $\eta / \epsilon$ . The first correction carries the Beta(3, 3) path weight $\rho _ { 2 }$ and has sign opposite to the leading term.

Tiny ϵ. Assume $\epsilon \ll t ( 1 - t ) \left| d _ { H } ( t ) \right|$ for all t and all H with $d _ { H } ( t ) \neq 0$ . Then eq. (30) reduces to the sign of the gradient, and the factor $t ( 1 - t )$ cancels:

$$
\Delta s _ { H } = - \eta \mathrm { s i g n } \big ( g _ { H } ( t ) \big ) = - \eta \mathrm { s i g n } \big ( d _ { H } ( t ) \big ) .\tag{34}
$$

Hence, with $t \sim \mathcal { U } ( 0 , 1 )$ ,

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \Delta s _ { H } ] = - \eta \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \mathrm { s i g n } \big ( d _ { H } ( t ) \big ) \mathrm { d } t + O ( 1 / N )\tag{35}
$$

$$
= \eta \Bigl ( \operatorname* { P r } _ { t } \bigl [ d _ { H } ( t ) < 0 \bigr ] - \operatorname* { P r } _ { t } \bigl [ d _ { H } ( t ) > 0 \bigr ] \Bigr ) + { \cal O } ( 1 / N ) .\tag{36}
$$

The magnitude of the $\mathbf { I } { \times } \mathbf { G }$ effect no longer matters, only how often along the path it is below the mean, i.e. how often restoring H lowers the loss by more than the average variable.

Path weights under general schedules. For a budget schedule with induced density $p ( t )$ on the path parameter, the first-step path weight is

$$
\rho _ { \mathrm { S G D } } ( t ) ~ \propto ~ p ( t ) t ( 1 - t ) , \qquad \rho _ { \mathrm { A d a m } , \epsilon \to 0 } ( t ) ~ = ~ p ( t ) ,\tag{37}
$$

since the gate slope $\sigma ^ { \prime } = t ( 1 - t )$ survives in the SGD (and large-ϵ) update but is normalised away by the sign. For the uniform, log-uniform, and logit schedules this gives ρ<sub>SGD</sub> $\in \{ 6 t ( 1 - t ) , 2 ( 1 - t ) ,$ 1} and $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { \rho _ { \mathrm { A d a m } , \epsilon \to 0 } } \in \{ 1 , \ \frac { 1 } { t \ln N } , \ \propto \frac { 1 } { t ( 1 - t ) } \} } \end{array}$ respectively. The log-uniform weight lives on $[ 1 / N , 1 ]$ and cannot be extended to $t = 0$ , since $\int _ { 0 } { \frac { \mathrm { d } t } { t } }$ diverges; the logit weight likewise needs a truncated interval $[ \delta , 1 - \delta ]$ for normalisability.

## A.4 MAttr IS NEITHER COMPLETE NOR LINEAR

In the setting of Bilodeau et al. (2024), the features of MAttr are the mask coordinates $\alpha ,$ the model is the intervened loss $\ell ( \alpha )$ of §A.2, the example is the base run $\alpha = 1$ and the baseline is the source run $\alpha = 0$ . Completeness would require $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { H } s _ { H } = \ell ( \mathbf { 1 } ) - \ell ( \mathbf { 0 } ) } \end{array}$ . Linearity would require that, for an additive $\begin{array} { r } { \ell ( { \pmb \alpha } ) = \sum _ { H } \ell ^ { ( H ) } ( \alpha _ { H } ) , s _ { H } } \end{array}$ equal the score MAttr assigns to $H$ when run on $\ell ^ { ( H ) }$ alone. Both fail because sigmoid top-k only ever compares scores to each other.

Not complete. $\mathsf { \ s i g T o p } { \mathsf K } _ { k } ( { \mathsf S } + c { \mathsf { 1 } } ) = \mathsf { S i g T o p } { \mathsf { K } } _ { k } ( { \mathsf { S } } )$ for every $c \in \mathbb { R }$ : the threshold $c _ { k }$ is the unique solution of $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { H } \sigma ( s _ { H } + c _ { k } ) = k _ { : } } \end{array}$ , so shifting every score by c shifts $c _ { k } \ b \ y - c$ and leaves the mask unchanged. Adding c to every score thus changes $\sum _ { H } s _ { H }$ by Nc without changing anything MAttr computes. The sum of the scores is a free constant, not $\ell ( \mathbf { 1 } ) - \ell ( \mathbf { 0 } )$ . (Under gradient descent it stays exactly 0, since $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { H } \partial \ell / \partial s _ { H } = 0 . ) } \end{array}$

Not linear. Run MAttr on a single variable H. For any budget k the constraint $\sigma ( s _ { H } + c _ { k } ) = k$ fixes the mask at k whatever $s _ { H }$ is, so $\partial \ell / \partial s _ { H } = 0$ and $s _ { H }$ never leaves its initialisation of 0. Linearity would thus force every MAttr score to be 0.

## C DETAILED DEFINITIONS OF ATTRIBUTION METHODS

We define and discuss all the attribution methods we experiment with on MIB. Similarly to MAttr, all methods produce scalar importance scores for each variable of interest in the model.

## C.1 GRADIENT-BASED ATTRIBUTION METHODS

Input times gradients (I×G). I×G uses a first-order Taylor approximation of the output around the base input to estimate the effect of varying the input:

$$
s _ { H } ^ { \mathrm { l x G } } = ( h ( \mathbf { b } ) - h ( \mathbf { s } ) ) \frac { \partial \ell ( \mathbf { x } ) } { \partial H } \Big | _ { \mathbf { x } = \mathbf { b } }\tag{38}
$$

Shrikumar et al. (2017) introduced I×G as a simple and better-performing alternative to directly using the gradient for attribution, as done in prior saliency map literature. This method has been rediscovered and renamed in the mechanistic interpretability literature as attribution patching (Nanda, 2023; Syed et al., 2024), and is also equivalent to single-step Integrated Gradients.

Integrated gradients (IG). IG (Sundararajan et al., 2017) extends the notion of Shapley values (defined on discrete variables) to continuous space via a path integral over the straight line between the input of interest b (i.e. ‘base’) and the baseline s (i.e. ‘source’). In practice, this quantity is approximated with a Riemann sum with m steps:

$$
{ \bf x } ^ { ( i ) } = \frac { i } { m } { \bf b } + \left( 1 - \frac { i } { m } \right) { \bf s }\tag{39}
$$

$$
h ^ { ( i ) } = h \left( \mathbf { x } ^ { ( i ) } \right)\tag{40}
$$

Initially, IG was only defined for input variables. The operationalisation of IG on non-input variables varies in the mechanistic interpretability literature; NAP-IG as defined in MIB uses the following (technically mathematically incorrect) expression, derived from the edge-level EAP-IG defined in Hanna et al. (2024).

$$
s _ { H } ^ { \mathsf { N A P - I G } } = ( h ( \mathbf { b } ) - h ( \mathbf { s } ) ) \frac { 1 } { m } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { \partial \ell ( \mathbf { x } ) } { \partial H } \Big | _ { \mathbf { x } = \mathbf { x } ^ { ( i ) } }\tag{41}
$$

The correct operationalisation is conductance (Dhamdhere et al., 2019), which applies the chain rule to estimate per-variable IG, and can be simplified as

$$
s _ { H } ^ { \mathsf { C o n d u c t a n c e } } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \left( h ^ { ( i ) } - h ^ { ( i - 1 ) } \right) \left. \frac { \partial \ell ( { \bf x } ) } { \partial H } \right| _ { { \bf x } = { \bf x } ^ { ( i ) } }\tag{42}
$$

The only difference from NAP-IG is that the delta term is moved inside the sum. The derivation is given in Shrikumar et al. (2018). In practice, these seem to strongly agree on MIB, suggesting that the per-step activation delta when interpolating inputs does not vary substantially in Transformer language models.

Expected gradients (EG). EG (Erion et al., 2021) is a simple modification of IG which only requires one backward pass per example. We modify the original algorithm: instead of using the marginal distribution over prompts as baselines, we use the counterfactual pairs.

$$
s _ { H } ^ { \mathsf { E G } } = \mathbb { E } _ { \langle \mathbf { b } , \mathbf { s } \rangle \sim \mathcal { D } , \alpha \sim \mathcal { U } ( 0 , 1 ) } \left[ \left( h ( \mathbf { b } ) - h ( \mathbf { s } ) \right) \frac { \partial \ell ( \mathbf { x } ) } { \partial H } \Big | _ { \mathbf { x } = \mathbf { b } + \boldsymbol { \alpha } ( \mathbf { s } - \mathbf { b } ) } \right]\tag{43}
$$

Relevance patching (RelP). A whole body of work termed Layerwise Relevance Propagation (LRP; Bach et al., 2015) proposes relevance conservation rules which propagate credit for some output metric backwards through the layers of the neural network, such that total credit is constant per-layer. RelP (Jafari et al., 2025) operationalises this by modifying backpropagation rules in language model computations as in the following table: RelP then computed I×G with this modified backward pass. Arora et al. (2026b;a) show that RelP is effective for finding causally-effective MLP neurons in language models for a variety of tasks.

RelP+QK. We introduce a variant of RelP where we remove the Attention rule and thus allow normal backpropagation via the QK path. Note that this breaks the relevance conservation property of RelP. This represents an intermediate technique between RelP and AttnLRP.

<table><tr><td>Operator</td><td>Linearisation</td></tr><tr><td>LayerNorm</td><td> $\underline { { \boldsymbol { x } _ { i } - \mathbb { E } [ \mathbf { x } ] } }$ </td></tr><tr><td></td><td> $\mathsf { d e t a c h } ( \sqrt { \epsilon + \mathrm { V a r } [ \mathbf { x } ] } )$ </td></tr><tr><td>SiLU/GELU</td><td> $\mathbf { x } \odot \mathsf { d e t a c h } ( \Phi ( \mathbf { x } ) )$ </td></tr><tr><td>Attention</td><td> $\sum _ { i } { \mathrm { d e t a c h } } ( A _ { i j } ) . \mathbf { v } _ { i }$ </td></tr><tr><td>Gating</td><td> $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( \mathbf { \dot { x } } \odot g ( \mathbf { x } ) ) + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathsf { d e t a c h } ( \mathbf { x } \odot g ( \mathbf { x } ) ) } \end{array}$ </td></tr></table>

AttnLRP. AttnLRP (Achtibat et al., 2024) is another LRP variant for Transformers which handles the QK path in Attention using the same half rule as in gating. The complete list of rules is similar to RelP, except for the Attention changes:
<table><tr><td>Operator</td><td>Linearisation</td></tr><tr><td>Attention (QK)</td><td> $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \frac { \mathbf q _ { j } \cdot \mathbf k _ { i } } { \sqrt d } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathsf d { e } \mathsf t a c h } \end{array}$   $\left( \frac { \mathbf { q } _ { j } \cdot \mathbf { k } _ { i } } { \sqrt { d } } \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Softmax</td><td> $\mathbf { \Phi } _ { A _ { i j } } = \operatorname { s o f t m a x } ( \mathbf { s } _ { j } ) _ { i }$  (unmodified)</td></tr><tr><td>Attention (AV)</td><td> $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i } A _ { i j } \cdot \mathbf { v } _ { i } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$  detach  $( \sum _ { i } A _ { i j } \cdot \mathbf { v } _ { i } )$ </td></tr></table>

RelP+Shapley. We experiment with a relevance-conserving rule for the softmax operation inside Attention, as an alternative to RelP and AttnLRP. This redistributes credit linearly based on the post-softmax scores $A _ { i j }$ , similarly to how other nonlinearities are handled in LRP.
<table><tr><td>Operator</td><td>Linearisation</td></tr><tr><td>Attention (QK)</td><td> $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \frac { \mathbf { q } _ { j } \cdot \mathbf { k } _ { i } } { \sqrt { d } } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathsf { d e t a c h } \left( \frac { \mathbf { q } _ { j } \cdot \mathbf { k } _ { i } } { \sqrt { d } } \right) } \end{array}$ </td></tr><tr><td>Softmax</td><td> $\widetilde { R } _ { i j } = \frac { A _ { i j } } { s _ { i j } } \sum _ { i ^ { \prime } } A _ { i ^ { \prime } j } R _ { i ^ { \prime } j }$  (backward rule)</td></tr><tr><td>Attention (AV)</td><td> $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i } A _ { i j } \cdot \mathbf { v } _ { i } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$  detach  $( \sum _ { i } A _ { i j } \cdot \mathbf { v } _ { i } )$ </td></tr></table>

GIM. Edin et al. (2026) claim that a major reason gradient-based attribution in LMs fails is that the attention softmax gradient is easily saturated. They propose adjusting the softmax temperature for the backward, along with the layernorm, gating, and half-rules like RelP.
<table><tr><td>Operator</td><td>Linearisation</td></tr><tr><td>SiLU/GELU</td><td> $\mathbf { x } \odot \Phi ( \mathbf { x } )$  (unmodified)</td></tr><tr><td>Attention (QK)</td><td> $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \frac { \mathbf q _ { j } \cdot \mathbf k _ { i } } { \sqrt d } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$   $\left( \frac { \mathbf { q } _ { j } \cdot \mathbf { k } _ { i } } { \sqrt { d } } \right)$  detach</td></tr><tr><td>Softmax</td><td> $A _ { i j } ^ { T } +$  detach  $\left( A _ { i j } - A _ { i j } ^ { T } \right)$   $A _ { i j } ^ { T } = \mathrm { s o f t m a x } ( \mathbf { s } _ { j } / T ) _ { i } , T = 2$ </td></tr><tr><td>Attention (AV)</td><td> $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i } A _ { i j } \cdot \mathbf { v } _ { i } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$  detach  $( \sum _ { i } { A _ { i j } } \cdot \mathbf { v } _ { i } )$ </td></tr></table>

## C.2 MASK-LEARNING METHODS

For all mask-learning methods, we define latent scores s, explain how the mask z is computed based on that, and patch the forward pass using the mask scores, where higher-scoring variables retain more of their original computation and lower-scoring ones are patched.

DBM. Differentiable binary masking is a broad family of mask-learning methods with somewhat confusing acronyms. We use a very simple version from the pyvene (Wu et al., 2024b) library, using a sigmoid with temperature parameter τ :

$$
{ \bf z } = \sigma ( { \bf s } / \tau )\tag{44}
$$

We anneal temperature from 50 to 0.1 over training. We optionally add an L1 penalty $( s \cdot \mathsf { m e a n } ( \mathbf { z } ) )$ on z to induce sparsity.

Edge pruning. Edge Pruning (Bhaskar et al., 2024) learns a binary mask over edges, i.e. residualstream terms corresponding to interactions between architectural components in a Transformer, just as in MIB’s edge circuits. It incorporates several known and new techniques for stabilising and improving mask learning; this makes it a strong baseline to compare MAttr against. We describe their algorithm below, following their given description and code.

The mask weights z are parametrised as a function of the latent scores S noised via the hard concrete distribution (Louizos et al., 2018; Xia et al., 2022):

$$
\mathbf { u } \sim \mathrm { U n i f o r m } ( \epsilon , 1 - \epsilon )\tag{45}
$$

$$
\mathbf { s } = \sigma \left( { \frac { 1 } { \beta } } \cdot \log { \frac { \mathbf { u } } { 1 - \mathbf { u } } } + \log \mathbf { S } \right)\tag{46}
$$

$$
{ \widetilde { \mathbf { s } } } = 1 . 2 \mathbf { s } - 0 . 1\tag{47}
$$

$$
{ \bf z } = \operatorname* { m i n } ( 1 , \operatorname* { m a x } ( 0 , \widetilde { { \bf s } } ) )\tag{48}
$$

For training, a target sparsity t is encouraged via a sparsity loss, where the parameters $\lambda _ { 1 } , \lambda _ { 2 }$ are gradient-ascented Lagrange multipliers (Wang et al., 2020) and s is the estimated L0 sparsity of scores:

$$
\mathcal { L } _ { s } = \lambda _ { 1 } ( t - s ) + \lambda _ { 2 } ( t - s ) ^ { 2 }\tag{49}
$$

The loss is summed to the actual target loss. Based on their code, we linearly warmup the sparsity term for 83% of training, and warmup overall LR for 7% of training. Bhaskar et al. (2024) use KL divergence from the unpruned model’s output distribution as the task loss, but we additionally experiment with label logit difference for better comparison to other methods on MIB.

We extend Edge Pruning to node-level attribution by learning the mask on component representations directly instead of the residual terms, which we term Node Pruning.

## D ALGORITHM, OPTIMISER, AND k-SCHEDULE ABLATIONS FOR MAttr

## D.1 SGD MATCHES ADAM ON NODE-LEVEL MIB

Compared to SGD, Adam has a 3× memory overhead for tracking the running mean and variance of every learnable parameter during optimisation. This can be costly when applying mask-learning attribution to fine-grained variable sets (e.g. MLP neurons, or individual model parameters), especially compared to gradient-based attribution methods which never use Adam.

Furthermore, the choice of optimiser fundamentally changes what MAttr learns. In §A.2 we showed that, under SGD, the first step of MAttr is equivalent to path-weighted Integrated Gradients, in expectation. This property does not hold under Adam due to its per-parameter update normalisation.

We therefore compare performance when optimising several variants of MAttr with Adam and SGD. Our results here focus on the representation attribution benchmark from the main text, and we sweep learning rates to give both optimisers a fair chance.

Result. For node-level representation attribution (see §4 for details), we find in Figure 6 that SGD and Adam achieve comparable performance on both CPR and Compactness, albeit at different learning rates. In both cases, a uniform k-schedule performs better on CPR and worse on Compactness. Note that the whole range of tested LRs shows similar Compactness, but greater variation in CPR.

![](images/7cf4ee1283ba802087696f5162f6cab438a4c66e24c48339ce5bf8aec81d0266.jpg)

![](images/83c7244fb423c0ab98cb2b2a8238b2358a7991c867ea61efd47192c365445898.jpg)  
Figure 6: Learning rate vs. average MIB validation scores, for MAttr with standard settings.

## D.2 ABLATIONS TO SIGMOID TOP-k AND k-SCHEDULE

We experiment with ablations to the MAttr algorithm in order to validate our design. Prior mask learning methods largely use non-differentiable hard masks with some straight-through estimator (Bengio et al., 2013) to enable backpropagation. We therefore experiment with:

$- c _ { k } \colon$ Sigmoid top-k forward and backward, but apply stop-gradient to the $c _ { k }$ term.

• +hard: Hard top-k forward, with sigmoid top-k as a straight-through estimator for the backward.

• +id-STE: Hard top-k forward with an identity straight-through estimator backward, i.e. directly backpropagation gradient to scores. This is LR-invariant.

• +Gum.: Add Gumbel noise to the forward pass only.

• +hard bwd: Hard-concrete backward.

Additionally, we compare using a log-uniform k-schedule or a uniform k-schedule, as well as Adam vs. SGD as above.

We show results in Figure 7, with average metrics over MIB node-level tasks as bars and the specific score for qwen/ioi indicated as a line due to the instability in performance we observe on that specific task. Broadly, we find that sigmoid top-k for both forward and backward performs best; the hard top-k forward and identity straight-through estimator both find pathological orderings specifically on ioi/qwen. Additionally, uniform-k results in higher CPR but slightly lower Compactness than log-k; we use uniform-k in the main text.

![](images/b224dbacd24aa80340a19eb85b4819c24e711d35ca43d994311cc0c0a95b066f.jpg)

![](images/113f0dfda993a5de34ee20af13dd40459f224de00d363eac459d06eaf0bfd336.jpg)  
Figure 7: Average MIB validation scores, along with specific score on qwen/ioi, for every MAttr ablation. Top text is learning rate.

## D.3 TUNING ϵ AND LR FOR ADAM AT GRANULAR BASES

Surprisingly, when using the default value of $\epsilon = 1 0 ^ { - 8 }$ for MAttr with Adam, we find that it actually performs worse than SGD on more granular bases (e.g. MLP neurons, MLP-output SAEs) but not so on node-level or edge-level MIB.

We were able to fix the MLP neuron-basis gap by tuning ϵ jointly with learning rate, as recommended in Choi et al. (2020) for Adam, resulting in the best choice of $\epsilon = 1 0 ^ { - 2 }$ . We showed earlier that a large ϵ may be helpful because the second-moment normalisation of the update in Adam removes useful per-parameter magnitude information from the MAttr update; see our derivation in §A.3.

Figure 8 shows that for a single MLP neuron-level task (addition from Feucht et al., 2026), a larger choice of ϵ leads to large and stable Compactness across LRs, while maintaining or even growing the CPR, and recovering more similar rankings to IG and MAttr+SGD at low LRs (indicating that these conservative LRs end up at least matching those baseline, while higher LRs correctly diverge and obtain higher evaluation scores).

![](images/e5fba5f01fb1dd0d8f46f4a40c181951d5d3c4c5000b1106ec0be325f42e7dff.jpg)  
(a) Compactness and CPR; underlined cells beat both IG and MAttr+SGD on that basis.

![](images/0a1320c55bed399b7eb549fd89f61c534666f28323ba38f65b21fbaa030821af.jpg)  
(b) Spearman ρ of the learned ranking against IG and against MAttr+SGD at its own best learning rate.  
Figure 8: Sweep of learning rate vs. ϵ for MAttr (Adam, uniform k) on the addition task from Feucht et al. (2026), one row per basis.

![](images/2686e1afd31683779534368fcdd317fa0eb473f6dd880a270e2edac7b23079a7.jpg)  
(a) Comparison of MAttr under SGD and Adam over training, at varying granularities on 3 tasks.

![](images/c60e48d21cd4bb8508f0b499ed8694b08187cdb399761a2701031304b06ca061.jpg)  
(b) Evaluation metrics when continuing training for 4× longer on the addition task, with MAttr.  
Figure 9: Optimiser experiments on tasks from Feucht et al. (2026).

Full comparison on Feucht et al. (2026) tasks. We compare Adam and SGD for MAttr with log k-schedule, along with IG, on the four tasks from Feucht et al. (2026) for which the key MLP neurons in Llama 3.1 8B Instruct have been identified with causal interventions: addition, hours, month, and weekdays.

In Figure 9a we plot evaluation metrics for our experiments over train steps. We find that at the component level, both optimisers perform well and match IG early in training. Interestingly, at MLP and MLP+Attn granularities, Adam with $\epsilon = 1 0 ^ { - 8 }$ struggles to learn and lags behind even IG on the Compactness metric. SGD with the logit difference training objective meanwhile surpasses IG on CPR while matching it on Compactness.

We continue training for 4× more steps in Figure 9b. Both SGD and Adam show continued improvements; however, Adam with $\epsilon = \mathrm { \bar { 1 0 } ^ { - 2 } }$ immediately begins to outperform SGD on CPR while matching it on Compactness, whereas $\epsilon = 1 0 ^ { - 8 }$ never matches baselines on Compactness.

## E DETAILED RESULTS FOR MIB

## E.1 HYPERPARAMETER TUNING FOR MAttr AND BASELINES

MAttr and mask learning techniques have a learning rate parameter which must be tuned. Additionally, mask learning methods usually have a sparsity coefficient whose weight must be tuned as well. We sweep these hyperparameters on the validation set of MIB (Mueller et al., 2025).

Learning rate. We report average CPR and IIA log-AUC on MIB for each of the ablations of MAttr (left panels) and mask learning techniques (right), at varying LRs. Generally, mask learning techniques achieve high CPR but struggle to find sparse circuits for IIA log-AUC. MAttr variants are largely performant, but using sigmoid top-k for the backward directly (as opposed to hard masks with straight-through estimators) results in sparser circuits.

![](images/1da24e082a90b8d798e9991bbe03be6e458ae6d9221de31e129317973411bdc5.jpg)

![](images/770b1a9bc91db2c91006dc2801ed974c73134883fc31d7143454f4080583807c.jpg)  
Figure 10: Learning rate sweep summary.

Sparsity coefficient. Node Pruning and DBM both have sparsity coefficients: the target sparsity s (theoretical $\in \ [ 0 , 1 ]$ , empirically allowed to go beyond that range), and the L1 coefficient (unbounded), respectively. We sweep these at a fixed LR due to compute constraints, train for 3000 steps (significantly more than gradient-based methods or MAttr) and plot results in Figure 11. We find that DBM is less sensitive to sparsity coefficient, but both methods achieve high CPR with the right sparsity coefficient. However, their Compactness is far below MAttr at all tested hyperparameter settings.

![](images/51d26b1de645202ad376789817694c57494cff94961cfb6be53e6c98470f922e.jpg)  
(a) CPR

![](images/282744d2896151852f11d27fa4ad44ed5afef2d13fbd164f2cd5272ae9ac4761.jpg)

![](images/bdff861c7d89c1aa2c368222565d4c08fb254cb351613783794a3dde2ce65080.jpg)  
Figure 11: Sparsity sweep summary.

![](images/7816bb1abe581b8df9ff1f6ce82b7fbb625ef5b69db54a64068314ceb32129b3.jpg)  
(b) Compactness

## E.2 MULTI-SPARSITY EVALUATION OF MASK-LEARNING METHODS

For each task, we train DBM and Node Pruning at varying sparsities to select the best sparsity to report. However, one may also combine the multiple different-sparsity runs into a single metric. To do this, we take the evaluation grid for our MIB metrics and, for each point, pick the empirical run for which the actual L0 is less than or equal to the grid point target sparsity, and compute Faithfulness and IIA using that (defaulting to 0 if no such point exists).

This multi-sparsity sweep results in much higher CPR and Compactness scores, at much higher cost than gradient-based attribution baselines or MAttr. Ultimately, multi-sparsity DBM and Node Pruning underperform MAttr. We report multi-sparsity scores on the MIB test set below.

![](images/bccebca522498a2509b5405bb3e58d2c5cb103a466fbaa052d040d787df5e873.jpg)  
Figure 12: Per-task MIB test scores of the multi-sparsity sweeps, compared to the best single-run scores of that method (Node Pruning s=0.5, DBM λ=6).  
Nestedness. MAttr enforces a nested ordering over the attributed variables. Multi-sparsity mask learning methods have no such property; a lower-L0 run need not be fully included in a higher-L0 run. We can empirically measure nestedness: for a given task, we average over every pair of sparsities the portion of lower-L0 variables are included in the higher-L0 run. In Table 1, DBM has an average nestedness of 0.97 and NP has 0.90.

## G VARIANT REPRESENTATION ATTRIBUTION EXPERIMENTS

Instead of the default interchange intervention and logit difference training and evaluation setup, we experiment with different ablations (zero ablation) and loss functions (CE, soft accuracy).

## G.1 ATTRIBUTION LOSS

We compare three choices of the training loss $\mathcal { L } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } , y )$ for training MAttr, each computed from the intervened logits $\hat { y } ^ { ( k ) }$ :

$$
\mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { L o g i t D i f f } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } , y ) = - \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { M I B } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } , y )\tag{50}
$$

$$
\mathcal { L } _ { \mathsf { C E } } ( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } , y ) = - \log \left( \left[ \operatorname { s o f t m a x } \left( \hat { y } ^ { ( k ) } \right) \right] _ { y _ { b } } \right)\tag{51}
$$

$$
\mathcal { L } _ { \sf S o f t A c c } ( \hat { \boldsymbol { y } } ^ { ( k ) } , \boldsymbol { y } ) = - \sigma \big ( \mathcal { L } _ { \sf M I B } ( \hat { \boldsymbol { y } } ^ { ( k ) } , \boldsymbol { y } ) \big )\tag{52}
$$

Table 8 shows CPR and Compactness for a set of node-level MIB and MIB+ tasks under each loss function. Logit difference generally seems to be the best loss across both metrics.
